  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because my eating has gotten really out of hand, especially in the evenings. I’ll stop and buy, like, a family-size takeout order—sometimes it’s enough for several people—and then I’ll pick up chips or something from the bakery too. And then I get home and eat most of it myself before it even really clicks how much is gone.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say. I supervise people all day and keep everything organized at work, you know, schedules and paperwork and everyone’s little emergencies, but then I get home and it’s like… I don’t know, I’m not paying attention until the food is almost gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, that’s exactly what it feels like. Once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips, and I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop, you don’t need more of this, and my hands are still opening the next container like I’m not the one in charge.\n\nIt sounds dramatic, I know. I’m usually the person at work reminding everyone else to follow the plan, and then with this it’s like the plan just disappears. Like I’m watching myself do it and not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I don’t sit there and name it, like “this is shame” or whatever. Usually I’m just kind of stunned afterward, like I look at the containers and wrappers and think, how did that happen again?\n\nAnd then I start fussing with cleaning it up, taking the trash out so it’s not just sitting there. Which is silly, because I live alone, it’s not like someone’s coming in to inspect my kitchen. Well, except my sister did once stop by unannounced and I about jumped out of my skin—not because of food, just because the place was a mess and she always has opinions about everything.\n\nSo… guilty? Maybe. Embarrassed, definitely. But I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating faster during these episodes than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, normally I’m not like that. At work I’m the one eating a salad at my desk between phone calls, or I’ll take forever with coffee because someone keeps interrupting me.\n\nBut during those times at home, it’s… fast. Really fast. I’ll be standing at the dining room table with the microwave still humming or the takeout boxes open everywhere, and I’m swallowing one bite while I’m already reaching for the next one. I barely taste it. It’s more like just getting it in, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these eating episodes by purging, exercising, or restricting your food intake on the following days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… not purging, if you mean making myself throw up. I don’t do that.\n\nExercise—well, I’ll tell myself I should go walk or something, but then it turns into this whole thing where I’m mad at myself because my sneakers are by the door and I haven’t used them in weeks, and then I remember I need to answer emails, and… anyway. I don’t know that I’m “making up for it” exactly.\n\nRestricting food the next day… sometimes I’ll think, okay, just be normal today, don’t make it worse. But I don’t know if that counts. I’m not very good at sorting what’s intentional versus just feeling off afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up. I mean, I’ve felt sick afterward sometimes, like just uncomfortable and upset with myself, but I don’t go to the bathroom and try to do that.\n\nI had a cousin years ago who used to talk about stomach problems all the time—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But no, that’s not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Um… laxatives or diuretics… I don’t think so? I mean, I know what laxatives are, sort of, but diuretics—are those like water pills?\n\nI’ve never really thought of that as part of this. Afterward I’m usually just kind of… upset and trying to get the kitchen cleaned up before I have to look at it too long. And then I get distracted by the mail on the counter or I remember something from work I didn’t finish.\n\nSo, no, not that I’m aware of. Unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, my weight has gone up and down over the years, but I can’t say exactly that it’s because of those episodes.\n\nI don’t weigh myself regularly—honestly, the scale in my bathroom has a laundry basket in front of it half the time, and then I’ll forget about it until I’m looking for something else. Clothes fitting differently, maybe, but then I start thinking maybe they shrunk in the wash or it’s just the brand… I’m probably avoiding knowing for sure.\n\nSo… significant changes? I’m not sure. I haven’t tracked it in a way where I could give you a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: Um… more than I’d like to admit, I guess. When I look back through my journal—because I do write things down, though not always in the most organized way, sometimes it’s mixed in with grocery lists or reminders about work—there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once.\n\nAnd most weeks… it’s several nights. I hate saying that out loud, but yeah, it’s not just occasional.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean—no, I don’t feel in control. That’s the scary part.\n\nOnce I start, it’s like this awful switch flips, and I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop, just put the lid back on… and my hands are still opening containers. Like I’m watching myself do it, but I’m not really the one in charge.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice something ridiculous, like the recycling needs to go out or the dishwasher is still full, and part of my brain goes there, but I’m still eating. So… yes, I know what I should do in the moment, but it doesn’t feel like I can actually make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's definitely larger than what most people would eat in a similar time period during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I try to talk myself out of calling it that, like “well, maybe I was just really hungry,” but no. It’s definitely more than most people would eat in that amount of time.\n\nLike I’ll buy what’s basically a family-size takeout order—because sometimes I tell myself I’ll save leftovers, and I even ask for extra containers like that makes it normal—and then I’ll stop at the store too and get chips or something from the bakery. And I’ll eat most of it myself in one evening before it even really hits me how much is gone.\n\nThen I look at the bags and containers afterward and it’s like… oh. That was not a normal dinner.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this way when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s always because I’m stressed or upset. I mean, work can be a lot—being the person everyone comes to with scheduling problems and “quick questions” that are never quick—but I don’t know if I’ve really tracked it that way.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, oh, I had a hard day, that must be why. But other times it’s not obvious. I might just be home, doing normal evening stuff, and then suddenly I’m in that pattern. So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed one clear emotional trigger every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe? It’s hard to separate it from just feeling overwhelmed afterward.\n\nSometimes I’m more focused on cleaning everything up, because if the kitchen looks normal then maybe I can sort of pretend the evening didn’t go off the rails. Like I’ll take the trash out right away, wipe the counters, straighten things. I’m like that at work too—if my desk is organized, I can convince myself I’m on top of things, even when everybody’s asking me for ten different things at once.\n\nBut after, I do sit there and think, why did I do that? So… I’m not sure. Maybe guilt, maybe just feeling bad about it.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do you have one of these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I started writing it down because I kept thinking maybe I was exaggerating, like maybe it just felt more frequent than it was. I’m the kind of person who keeps lists for everything—work schedules, errands, even what’s in the freezer—and sometimes that makes me feel more in control.\n\nBut looking back through my journal… there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. And honestly, most weeks it’s several times. I don’t like saying that out loud, but that’s what it looks like when I actually count it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the part that scares me the most.\n\nOnce I start, it’s like this awful switch flips. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, you don’t even want this anymore, and then my hands are still opening containers or getting more food like I’m not the one in charge. It sounds ridiculous saying it, because at work I’m the one who keeps everything moving and tells people what needs to happen next, but at home in that moment I just… I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes because you're eating in response to an emotional state, or is it more like your eating is on autopilot?\n\nPatient: It feels more like autopilot, I think. I mean, sometimes I’m upset before it happens, but once I start, it’s like this switch flips.\n\nI’ll be thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop, you don’t even want this, and then my hands are still opening containers or grabbing more like I’m not really the one making the decisions. It’s strange because I can control so many little things at work—forms, schedules, who’s covering phones—but in that moment it’s like I’m not in charge of my own hands.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating during these episodes is more in response to how you're feeling emotionally, or is it more like you're not really thinking about your emotions at all?\n\nPatient: I think it’s less about me sitting there feeling something and more like I’m not really thinking at all once it starts. I mean, maybe something got me keyed up earlier, but in the actual moment it’s like this awful switch flips.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, you’ve had enough, and then my hands are still opening containers or getting more food like they didn’t get the message. It’s embarrassing because I’m usually the person keeping track of everything, but right then I don’t feel like I’m the one in charge.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're criticizing yourself or thinking about how much you're eating while you're in the middle of one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do criticize myself, but it’s almost like it doesn’t do anything. Like I’ll be right in the middle of it thinking, Taylor, put it away, what are you doing, and then I’m still reaching for another container.\n\nIt’s strange because I can be so aware for a second—like I know this is too much, I know I should stop—and then my hands just keep going anyway. I keep thinking of it like a switch flips, and suddenly I’m not really the one in charge. I hate saying that because it sounds dramatic, and I’m not usually like that. I’m the person at work who notices if someone filed something in the wrong folder, you know? But with this, in the moment, the thoughts are there and they just don’t seem to matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no control over your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s probably the scariest part of it. I’m like that at work too—if my desk is organized, I can convince myself I’m on top of things, even when everybody’s asking me for ten different things at once.\n\nBut after, I do sit there and think, why did I do that? So… I’m not sure. Maybe guilt, maybe just feeling bad about it.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do you have one of these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I started writing it down because I kept thinking maybe I was exaggerating, like maybe it just felt more frequent than it was. I’m the kind of person who keeps lists for everything—work schedules, errands, even what’s in the freezer—and sometimes that makes me feel more in control.\n\nBut looking back through my journal… there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. And honestly, most weeks it’s several times. I don’t like saying that out loud, but that’s what it looks like when I actually count it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the part that scares me the most.\n\nOnce I start, it’s like this awful switch flips. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, you don’t even want this anymore, and then my hands are still opening containers or getting more food like I’m not the one in charge. It sounds ridiculous saying it, because at work I’m the one who keeps everything moving and tells people what needs to happen next, but at home in that moment I just… I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes because you're eating in response to an emotional state, or is it more like your eating is on autopilot?\n\nPatient: It feels more like autopilot, I think. I mean, sometimes I’m upset before it happens, but once I start, it’s like this switch flips.\n\nI’ll be thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop, you don’t even want this, and then my hands are still opening containers or grabbing more like I’m not really the one making the decisions. It’s strange because I can control so many little things at work—forms, schedules, who’s covering phones—but in that moment it’s like I’m not in charge of my own hands.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating during these episodes is more in response to how you're feeling emotionally, or is it more like you're not really thinking about your emotions at all?\n\nPatient: I think it’s less about me sitting there feeling something and more like I’m not really thinking at all once it starts. I mean, maybe something got me keyed up earlier, but in the actual moment it’s like this awful switch flips.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, Taylor, put it away, you’ve had enough, and then my hands are still opening containers or getting more food like they didn’t get the message. It’s embarrassing because I’m usually the person keeping track of everything, but right then I don’t feel like I’m the one in charge.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're criticizing yourself or thinking about how much you're eating while you're in the middle of one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do criticize myself, but it’s almost like it doesn’t do anything. Like I’ll be right in the middle of it thinking, Taylor, put it away, what are you doing, and then I’m still reaching for another container.\n\nIt’s strange because I can be so aware for a second—like I know this is too much, I know I should stop—and then my hands just keep going anyway. I keep thinking of it like a switch flips, and suddenly I’m not really the one in charge. I hate saying that because it sounds dramatic, and I’m not usually like that. I’m the person at work who notices if someone filed something in the wrong folder, you know? But with this, in the moment, the thoughts are there and they just don’t seem to matter.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have no control over your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s probably the scariest part of it. Once I start, it’s like this awful switch flips, and even if I’m thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop, my hands just keep going.\n\nI’ll still be opening containers or reaching for more like I’m watching myself do it instead of deciding to do it. And I know that sounds odd—I supervise people all day, I’m used to being the one keeping things organized—but in that moment, no, I really don’t feel in control."
